[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 21.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"b04973","wall_time_seconds":1.1037709,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2532,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: LSAT Logical Reasoning Level 1 Answer Parsing & Mapping\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# LSAT Logical Reasoning: Level 1 Answer Parsing & Mapping\\r\\n\\r\\n**Student Name:** ________________ **Date:** ________________\\r\\n\\r\\n## Instructions\\r\\n\\r\\nTo master Method of Reasoning and Flaw questions, you must be able to \\"de-stractify\\" the answer choices. Do not just read these phrases; map them.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **The Conclusion** is the Destination (where the argument is going).\\r\\n- **The Evidence** is the \\"Method of Transportation\\" (how it gets there).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part I: The Vocabulary of Logic\\r\\n\\r\\n*Before mapping structure, you must define the terms accurately. In the context of the LSAT, define the strict logical meaning of the following terms.*\\r\\n\\r\\n| Term | LSAT Definition & Requirement |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| **1. \\"Explanation\\"** | *Hint: It does not mean \\"elaborating\\" or \\"providing detail.\\"* **Strict Definition:** _________________ |\\r\\n| **2. \\"Defend\\"** | *Hint: Stating a motivation is not a defense.* **Strict Definition:** _________________ |\\r\\n| **3. \\"Aesthetic Principle\\"** | *Hint: It is not just a specific opinion about one painting.* **Strict Definition:** _________________ |\\r\\n| **4. \\"Supported Premise\\"** | *Hint: It functions as both a roof and a floor.* **Also known as:** _________________ |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part II: Structural Mapping (X vs. Y)\\r\\n\\r\\n*For the following abstract answer choices, identifying the variable is not enough. You must identify the **Indicator Word** that tells you the direction of the logic.*\\r\\n\\r\\n### Task\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Circle** the Indicator Word (e.g., \\"because,\\" \\"from,\\" \\"by\\").\\r\\n2. Identify which variable represents the **Conclusion (C)** and which represents the **Evidence (E)**.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Abstract Phrase | Indicator Word | Conclusion | Evidence |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| *Example: infers that X on the grounds that Y* | *on the grounds that* | **X** | **Y** |\\r\\n| 1. doing **X** by doing **Y** | | | |\\r\\n| 2. using **X** to support **Y** | | | |\\r\\n| 3. concludes, from the claim that **X**, that **Y** | | | |\\r\\n| 4. concludes **X** on the basis of **Y** | | | |\\r\\n| 5. infers, on the grounds that **X**, that **Y** | | | |\\r\\n| 6. takes for granted that **Y** because **X** | | | |\\r\\n| 7. **Y** is true merely on the basis of **X** | | | |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Part III: Reverse Engineering & Context\\r\\n\\r\\n*In this section, we move from abstract variables (X/Y) to specific content. You must \\"reverse engineer\\" the answer choice to determine what the stimulus must look like.*\\r\\n\\r\\n**8. The Phrase:** *\\"Fails to consider that even if X, may not be Y\\"*\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Logical Translation:** This describes a Flaw. The argument assumes that if you have **X** (Evidence), you generally get **Y** (Conclusion/Effect).\\r\\n- **Write a Real-World Example:** Write a simple sentence using *raining* (X) and *getting wet* (Y) that fits this structure.\\r\\n- *Your Argument:* _________________\\r\\n\\r\\n**9. The Phrase:** *\\"Generalized too readily from the fruits it tested to the fruits it did not test\\"*\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Reverse Engineer the Stimulus:** For this answer choice to be correct, what **two specific things** must exist in the text of the stimulus?\\r\\n- *Requirement A (The Evidence):* _________________\\r\\n- *Requirement B (The Conclusion):* _________________\\r\\n\\r\\n**10. The Phrase:** *\\"Explaining a historical fact in terms of the artistic preferences of people\\"*\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Logical Translation:** Remember, \\"Explanation\\" means Causation.\\r\\n- **Identify the Cause and Effect:**\\r\\n- *The Cause (The Explainer):* _________________\\r\\n- *The Effect (The Result):* _________________\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n*Source: Google Doc `1GpKxUnK3JXGY9g-L7yMRtz8Jlm2bobgwG59ViFpzWSI` (the user\'s \\"improved form\\" of the worksheet). Categorized as **Abstract** — vocabulary calibration + structural mapping of abstract X/Y phrases + reverse engineering. Direct companion to the existing Advanced Parsing Answer Key.*\\r\\n---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: scan Role Questions Recap Drill\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n**📘 Recap – Role Questions Accuracy Drill**\\r\\n\\r\\n**\\\\\\r\\nLevel 3**\\r\\n\\r\\n**Correct answers on Role**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe correct answer doesn\'t say anything descriptively wrong. The other four do. (Maybe once in a blue moon, you\'d have two \\"true\\" answers, where you\'d have to ask which one\'s better.)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe most common correct answer is Premise or some other way of describing Supporting Idea.\\r\\n\\r\\nEasier Role questions will stick to classic language like Premise and Conclusion, whereas harder Role questions will often switch to less familiar abstract language or synonyms.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Main Conclusion –**\\r\\n\\r\\nthe claim the author seeks to establish, a generalization supported by evidence, the only conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Premise –**\\r\\n\\r\\nsupport, partial support (just means there\'s more than one premise), evidence, illustration of, example of, unsupported premise.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Intermediate Conclusion –**\\r\\n\\r\\nsubsidiary conclusion, supported premise, a conclusion but not the main conclusion, a statement for which some support is provided but is used to support the main conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Opposing Position –**\\r\\n\\r\\nthe view the argument is designed to discredit, the claim the author attempts to show is false.\\r\\n\\r\\n**\\\\\\r\\nIncorrect answers on Role**\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Wrong Ingredient –**\\r\\n\\r\\nthese describe something in the paragraph, but not the claim we were asked about.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Too Strong –**\\r\\n\\r\\nsome answers that are about an idea being required or sufficient for the conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Didn\'t Happen –**\\r\\n\\r\\nthese reference something that never happened in the paragraph.\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Wrong Conclusion –**\\r\\n\\r\\nlots of wrong answers correctly call the claim a Premise but then say it\'s supporting some idea that doesn\'t match the argument\'s Conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\n**🔹 To advance from Level 3 to Level 4:**\\r\\n\\r\\n• You must get 5 out of 5 correct in a row.\\r\\n\\r\\n• If you get a question wrong:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\- You must write at least 3–5 sentences explaining why you chose the wrong answer — what specifically attracted you to it and why it seemed appealing.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\- Then write another 3–5 sentences explaining what makes the right answer correct and why it may have initially seemed less appealing.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\- This reflection must be done untimed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**\\\\\\r\\nLevel 4**\\r\\n\\r\\nOn harder Role questions, we\'re likely to see a couple patterns emerge:\\r\\n\\r\\n**• Intermediate Conclusion?**\\r\\n\\r\\nMany of the harder problems test the existence of an Intermediate Conclusion, even if the claim we\'re being asked about isn’t itself that. Answer choices describing a Premise might differ by saying, “It provides direct support for the Conclusion” vs. “It provides support for a claim that then provides direct support for the Conclusion.”\\r\\n\\r\\nTo assess whether a claim is or isn’t an Intermediate Conclusion, we want to ask ourselves,\\r\\n\\r\\n*Is there Support for this claim? Why should we believe this claim? Does anything else in the paragraph sound like it\'s answering that question?*\\r\\n\\r\\nOften, when there isn\'t a supporting idea present and thus the claim you\'re examining is really just a Premise, it’s useful to think about how you would support that claim.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf you\'re asking yourself, “Was the claim You shouldn\'t tell jokes at a funeral supported?”—supply your own answer. Just get your brain hearing what actual support would sound like: “You shouldn\'t tell jokes at a funeral because... funerals are a somber occasion and telling jokes makes it seem like you\'re not respecting the gravity of the situation.” By articulating what support would sound like, you can sometimes help strengthen your conviction that no support was given.\\r\\n\\r\\n**🔹 To advance from Level 4 to Level 5:**\\r\\n\\r\\n• You must get 5 out of 5 correct in a row.\\r\\n\\r\\n• If you get a question wrong:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\- You must write at least 3–5 sentences explaining why you chose the wrong answer — what about it seemed right or tempting.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\- Then write another 3–5 sentences explaining why the correct answer is actually right, and what you may have overlooked or misunderstood.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\- This must be completed untimed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**\\\\\\r\\nLevel 5**\\r\\n\\r\\nFocus mainly on getting more comfortable with the abstract answers.\\r\\n---\\r\\ntype: homework\\r\\ntopic: role\\r\\ncluster_type: evolution_pile\\r\\nsource_count: 2\\r\\ntotal_words: 953\\r\\ngenerated_at: 2026-04-24T08:09:24.024457+00:00\\r\\nsources:\\r\\n  - {\\"path\\": \\"LSAT DUMP/LR/Match Family/Role/Homework ( Dissect LSAT arguments).pdf\\", \\"modified\\": \\"2024-07-08T21:43:37+00:00\\", \\"word_count\\": 623, \\"doc_id\\": null}\\r\\n  - {\\"path\\": \\"LSAT DUMP/LR/Match Family/Role/ROLE HOMEWORK PT 2.pdf\\", \\"modified\\": \\"2025-07-18T02:45:37.660000+00:00\\", \\"word_count\\": 330, \\"doc_id\\": null}\\r\\nsubject: Homework Role\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Homework ( Dissect LSAT arguments).pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Match Family/Role/Homework ( Dissect LSAT arguments).pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-08T21:43:37+00:00  **Words:** 623  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nWorksheet Instructions Use these instructions to dissect arguments and understand the relationships between statements. The words provided will help you lead your analysis: Key Elements 1. Conclusion: Words to look for: therefore, thus, accordingly, hence, consequently, it follows that, so 2. Support: Words to look for: because, for, since, as, given that 3. Pivots: Words to look for: but, yet, however 4. Opinion: Words to look for: should, clearly, obviously, rebuttals 5. Opposing Viewpoint: 6.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: ROLE HOMEWORK PT 2.pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Match Family/Role/ROLE HOMEWORK PT 2.pdf  **Modified:** 2025-07-18T02:45:37.660000+00:00  **Words:** 330  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\n• • • • • • • • • • • • • • • • • Step 1: Find the Conclusion The conclusion should sound like the author’s opinion. It must be supported by at least one explicit claim in the argument. Avoid mistaking facts for conclusions—conclusions are opinions or judgments based on facts. Make sure it’s not an Intermediate Conclusion (statements that are conclusions for some premises but also act as support for the main conclusion). Step 2: Find the Supporting Idea(s) Identify the premises or claims that di\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 6.5 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"9a54da","wall_time_seconds":0.6569266,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":971,"output":"\\r\\nName\\r\\n----\\r\\n03-art-of-matching-keywords.md\\r\\n# The Art of Matching Keywords : Strategy Doc\\r\\n\\r\\n> Source: `THE_ART_OF_MATCHING_KEYWORD__K__METHOD__FLAW___1_.pdf`\\r\\n> Status: VERBATIM. Strategy content for the chapter + reference cards.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Identifying Incorrect Descriptions (Art of Matching the Keywords)\\r\\n\\r\\nFor questions that require identifying features of the stimulus\' argument (Role, Method, Flaw), it is crucial to spot answer choices that describe something that did not happen in the argument. Here\'s how to approach these questions:\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Watchful Eye:** Carefully read the answer choices to ensure they accurately reflect the argument.\\r\\n- **Deviations from the Stimulus:** Eliminate choices that describe events or actions not present in the stimulus.\\r\\n- **Question Types:** This strategy is especially useful for Role, Method, and Flaw questions. However, keep in mind that a lot in Strengthen/Weaken questions might have correct answers that introduce new elements not directly stated in the stimulus.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Two-Step Framework\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Having a clear grasp of the premises, conclusions, and assumptions within an argument is crucial to success on Method questions. **(S : Structure)**\\r\\n2. We also need to have a clear and discrete understanding of the answer choices. Know what each keyword is referring to, and don\'t choose an answer choice if you can\'t match it up with the stimulus! **(K : Keywords)**\\r\\n\\r\\n> **IDENTIFY (S)TRUCTURE → (K)EYWORDS**\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## How To Guide (use this with additional keywords for the question)\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Read the Answer Choice Over:** Extract any keywords that stand out to you.\\r\\n2. **Analyze Keywords in LSAT Context:** Think about what these keywords mean in a LSAT-specific setting.\\r\\n3. **Check Keyword Relevance:**\\r\\n   - If it\'s a noun: Ask yourself, does this concept appear in the stimulus?\\r\\n   - If it\'s a verb: Ask yourself, does the author do this in the argument?\\r\\n   - If it\'s an adjective/adverb: Does it correctly describe the tone or strength of the author\'s argument?\\r\\n4. **Match with Stimulus Structure:** Read the answer choice again and see if you can match up what the answer is describing with the stimulus\' structure.\\r\\n5. **Practical Approach:** Don\'t be afraid to go back and forth between the answer choice and the stimulus. The more you practice this skill, the easier it will become.\\r\\n6. **Beware of Traps:** A common trick employed by the test authors is to provide an almost perfect answer with a glaring error, usually due to one single word. This word is placed right next to the correct answer, which is worded vaguely. This is a trap that you will encounter repeatedly throughout the test preparation.\\r\\n\\r\\n> We need to have a clear and discrete understanding of the answer choices. Know what each keyword is referring to, and don\'t choose an answer choice if you can\'t match it up with the stimulus!\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Definitions of Other Keywords\\r\\n\\r\\nThere is a difference between assumptions and implications. An assumption, as we spoke about in class, is an unstated premise or intermediate conclusion linking the supporting information and the main conclusion of an argument.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Assumption:** an unstated premise (sometimes answer choices will say sneaky things like IMPLICIT PREMISE, which is another way of saying assumption).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Implication:** something that will follow if you accept the truth of the conclusion. An unstated inference if you accept the truth of the argument.\\r\\n\\r\\nInferences and implications play a big role in MBT and MSS questions.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Logical Contradiction:** a state and its denial existing simultaneously.\\r\\n_archive\\r\\n_HANDOFF\\r\\n_source\\r\\nHomework\\r\\nNotes\\r\\n_AUDIT_2026-05-14.md\\r\\n_BUILD_SPEC_v2.0.md\\r\\n_DRILLS_AUDIT.md\\r\\n_PROJECT.md\\r\\nBOOTCAMP_DEPTH_AND_POLISH.md\\r\\nINDEX.generated.md\\r\\nLESSON_READABILITY_FIX.md\\r\\nMODULE_CLOSEOUT.md\\r\\nMODULE_PLAN.md\\r\\nMODULE_QUALITY_UPGRADE.md\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 5.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"eb9cbc","wall_time_seconds":0.508219,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4331,"output":"# Abstract Bootcamp: Module Plan\\r\\n\\r\\nThe bootcamp is 7 modules in a straight line. Each module has 3 parts: Note, Quiz, Drill. Finish a part to unlock the next part. Finish a module to unlock the next module. That is the whole structure.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The 7 modules\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 1: Why Abstract Is Hard\\r\\n- **Note:** Why abstract answer choices break test-takers.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 5 items. Read a stimulus and an answer choice, spot what makes the choice abstract.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 3 LSAT questions to feel the difficulty before learning the fix.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 2: LSAT Words You Must Know\\r\\n- **Note:** The 7 LSAT-specific terms with definitions and examples: Presuppose, Illustrate, General Principle, Explanation, Hypothesis, Phenomenon, Undermine.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 10 flashcard-style items. See the term, pick the LSAT meaning.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 3 LSAT questions where one of these terms decides the right answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 3: Stimulus + Keyword Extraction\\r\\n- **Note:** The core method. How to find the load-bearing words in an abstract answer choice and translate them into plain English using the stimulus as your anchor.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 15 items. For each abstract question stem, the student clicks the load-bearing keywords, types a plain-English translation, then sees the full walkthrough with vocab callouts, requirements, and worked example.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 5 LSAT questions where keyword extraction is the path to the right answer.\\r\\n\\r\\nNote for the data: Item 10 in `data/translatorItems.ts` currently has only 2 keywords. Expand it to 3-5 keywords like the others (suggested: \\"purports\\", \\"explain\\", \\"argument\\" or similar load-bearing words from the stem).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 4: Match To The Stimulus\\r\\n- **Note:** How to test each part of an abstract answer choice against the actual stimulus. If any part fails, the answer dies.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 10 items. Single-column flow. Read a stimulus, read an answer choice with its parts, decide which parts match and which do not.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 5 LSAT questions where matching is the path to the answer.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 5: Spot The Method\\r\\n- **Note:** The 17 method-of-reasoning patterns with plain-English names and stimulus examples.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 10 items. See a stimulus, pick the method pattern from 4 choices.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 5 method-of-reasoning LSAT questions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 6: Catch The Traps\\r\\n- **Note:** Common traps in abstract answers: one-word swaps, wrong role attribution, wrong method, structural mismatch.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 10 items. Spot the trap in a wrong answer choice.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 5 trap-heavy LSAT questions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Module 7: Put It Together\\r\\n- **Note:** Brief recap. The whole workflow in one place.\\r\\n- **Quiz:** 18 items. The full decoding flow: keywords, translate, match, choose. The current Decoding Gauntlet content.\\r\\n- **Drill:** 5 mixed LSAT questions.\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter Module 7: **Final Set** (5 PT questions, timed).\\r\\n\\r\\n## Inside a module\\r\\n\\r\\nOne page per module. Three rows. The current part has the big button. Done parts collapse to one line. Locked parts show a lock icon and the unlock requirement.\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nMODULE 3: STIMULUS + KEYWORD EXTRACTION\\r\\n\\r\\n[ ✓ ] Note         12 min          Done\\r\\n[ ● ] Quiz         15 items        8 of 15 correct, keep going\\r\\n[ 🔒 ] Drill        5 questions     Locked. Pass the quiz at 60% to unlock.\\r\\n\\r\\n[ Continue Quiz → ]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nQuizzes run one item at a time, single column. No three-column workspace. No simultaneous decisions. Read the prompt, do the interaction, see the reveal, move on.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sidebar\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nDashboard\\r\\n─────────\\r\\nModule 1\\r\\nModule 2\\r\\nModule 3\\r\\nModule 4\\r\\nModule 5\\r\\nModule 6\\r\\nModule 7\\r\\n─────────\\r\\nFinal Set\\r\\n─────────\\r\\nReference (collapsed by default)\\r\\n  Flashcards\\r\\n  Methods Library\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nDone modules show a checkmark. Current module is highlighted. Locked modules show a lock icon. That is the whole sidebar.\\r\\n\\r\\nOld entries for Extraction, Translator, Decoding Gauntlet, and Targeted Drills are removed. Those experiences now live inside the modules where they belong.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Gamification (subtle, polished, not gimmicky)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe student should feel forward motion without the app feeling like a game. Reach for satisfaction, not novelty.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **XP per part.** Note = 10 XP. Quiz = 1 XP per correct item. Drill = 5 XP per correct question. XP shows in the sidebar header next to a small star icon.\\r\\n- **Streak counter.** Track consecutive correct items inside a quiz or drill. Reset on wrong. Show \\"🔥 5 in a row\\" when streak is 3 or higher.\\r\\n- **Module progress ring.** Each module item in the sidebar has a small circular ring showing how complete it is. Empty ring on lock. Quarter ring after note. Three-quarter ring after quiz. Full filled ring after drill.\\r\\n- **Completion animation.** When a module hits 100%, play one short confetti burst over the module name in the sidebar. Half a second. Subtle.\\r\\n- **Achievement on perfect module.** If the student gets 100% on both quiz and drill in a single module, unlock an \\"Aced It\\" badge on the module tile in the dashboard. Small. Quiet.\\r\\n- **Streak persistence across sessions.** Daily streak counter at the top of the dashboard (\\"3-day streak\\"). Resets if a full calendar day passes with zero activity. Use a flame icon.\\r\\n\\r\\nNo leaderboards. No avatars. No mascots. No sound effects.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Design quality bar\\r\\n\\r\\nMatch the existing brutalist palette and tokens. Bring the polish up to these specs across every new and rebuilt component.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Type hierarchy.** Each screen has one clear primary header, one supporting subheader, then body. No competing emphasis.\\r\\n- **Spacing rhythm.** Vertical rhythm in 8px increments. Section gaps in 32px or 48px. Card padding 24px or 32px.\\r\\n- **Motion.** Stage transitions use spring easing, 280ms. Reveals fade and slide 8px. Respect `useReducedMotion`. No gratuitous animation.\\r\\n- **Touch targets.** All buttons minimum 44px height.\\r\\n- **Empty states.** Every list and panel has a designed empty state, not blank.\\r\\n- **Loading states.** Skeleton bones for content that takes longer than 200ms to load. Spinner only for actions, not for content.\\r\\n- **Mobile.** Sidebar collapses to a drawer below 768px. Module page stacks vertically. Quiz items remain single column, larger touch targets.\\r\\n- **Color discipline.** Only one accent color highlighted at a time on any screen. The brutal yellow is reserved for active states and primary CTAs.\\r\\n- **Iconography.** Lucide icons only. One stroke weight. No emoji except the streak flame and the lock.\\r\\n- **Copy tone.** Plain English. Direct. Second person. No exclamation points. No \\"Awesome!\\" or \\"Great job!\\" Use \\"Nice. Next.\\" instead.\\r\\n- **Accessibility.** Every interactive element has an `aria-label` or visible label. Focus states are visible. Color contrast meets WCAG AA.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Content mapping\\r\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:1:# Abstract Bootcamp: Module Plan\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:7:### Module 1: Why Abstract Is Hard\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:12:### Module 2: LSAT Words You Must Know\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:17:### Module 3: Stimulus + Keyword Extraction\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:24:### Module 4: Match To The Stimulus\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:29:### Module 5: Spot The Method\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:30:- **Note:** The 17 method-of-reasoning patterns with plain-English names and stimulus examples.\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:34:### Module 6: Catch The Traps\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/MODULE_PLAN.md:36:- **Quiz:** 10 items. Having a clear grasp of the premises, conclusions, and assumptions within an argument is crucial to success on Method questions. **(S : Structure)**\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:21:2. We also need to have a clear and discrete understanding of the answer choices. Know what each keyword is referring to, and don\'t choose an answer choice if you can\'t match it up with the stimulus! **(K : Keywords)**\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:27:## How To Guide (use this with additional keywords for the question)\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:30:2. **Analyze Keywords in LSAT Context:** Think about what these keywords mean in a LSAT-specific setting.\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:35:4. **Match with Stimulus Structure:** Read the answer choice again and see if you can match up what the answer is describing with the stimulus\' structure.\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:43:## Definitions of Other Keywords\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:45:There is a difference between assumptions and implications. An assumption, as we spoke about in class, is an unstated premise or intermediate conclusion linking the supporting information and the main conclusion of an argument.\\nraw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:49:**Implication:** something that will follow if you accept the truth of the conclusion. An unstated inference if you accept the truth of the argument.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Abstract/Homework/Level 1 Answer Parsing and Mapping.md:12:## Answer choices describing a Premise might differ by saying, “It provides direct support for the Conclusion” vs. “It provides support for a claim that then provides direct support for the Conclusion.”\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Role/Homework/_scan_Role_Questions_Recap_Drill.md:77:To assess whether a claim is or isn’t an Intermediate Conclusion, we want to ask ourselves,\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Role/Homework/_scan_Role_Questions_Recap_Drill.md:81:Often, when there isn\'t a supporting idea present and thus the claim you\'re examining is really just a Premise, it’s useful to think about how you would support that claim.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Role/Homework/_scan_Role_Questions_Recap_Drill.md:83:If you\'re asking yourself, “Was the claim You shouldn\'t tell jokes at a funeral supported?”—supply your own answer. Just get your brain hearing what actual support would sound like: “You shouldn\'t tell jokes at a funeral because... funerals are a somber occasion and telling jokes makes it seem like you\'re not respecting the gravity of the situation.” By articulating what support would sound like, you can sometimes help strengthen your conviction that no support was given.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:10:## I. Disqualification Strategy\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:44:## IV. Question Type Deep Dives\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:57:### A. Method of Reasoning (Argument Procedure)\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:62:  - *Trap:* Watch for answers that reverse the order (e.g., saying the argument uses X to support Y, when it actually uses Y to support X).\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:64:### B. Flaw Questions\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:66:- **Reverse Engineering:** Before reading the stimulus, scan the answer choices to see what each *would* require to be correct.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:71:## VI. Timing and Next Steps\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:73:### The Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:75:- **Disregard Speed:** Do not focus on speed right now. Prioritizing speed over accuracy leads to mistakes.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:76:- **The Fix:** Complete accuracy leads to intuition. Once you are accurate, efficiency naturally follows. Speed is a byproduct of mastery.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:78:### Homework & Drills\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:80:1. **Reverse Engineering:** For the next set of questions, look at the answer choices *first*. Identify exactly what would need to be in the stimulus for that answer to be correct.\\ncorpus/Curriculum/Method/Homework/Method and Flaw Strategy Guide.md:85:*Source: Google Doc `1IuVq6GDsxjqIUAqsRcAtFWfFRGN_YBPowKt_tnRHGjk`. Recommended lecture structure, as new synthesis for review:

1. Explain what an abstract description keeps: the statement’s job and relationships, with the subject matter removed.
2. Establish the important word definitions.
3. Demonstrate one original source choice: keywords → exact required features → a concrete argument containing them.
4. Explain why a plausible example can still fail if one requirement is absent or its direction/strength changes.
5. Give the exact source seven-step routine.
6. Introduce the 15 exercises and the learner’s two written tasks.
7. Close by showing how their constructed example prepares them to test a real stimulus: replace the invented content with the actual statements and verify every requirement. End the lesson there unless Joshua wants a separate application course.

Retire the mandatory M4/M5 continuation for this lesson; preserve useful matching teaching in the closing explanation and leave Methods as an optional, separately reviewed reference.

- Role recap lists “the only conclusion” as a main-conclusion synonym; that is not generally valid when intermediate conclusions exist.
- Method guide claims premise indicators “guarantee” evidence, advocates “least wrong,” and gives other broad heuristics. These are source claims requiring judgment, not approved universal rules.

Retirement-savings analogy (0.1% to 1% is ten times more and still too little). | C note is one line and does not say it deepens. | N |\\r\\n\\r\\n### What the best Paradox explanations do\\r\\n\\r\\n- **They name the expectation and the kind of fix before the choices.** PT148-S3-Q11 says the known difference (surveys) cannot be the cause, so the answer must be a second difference between the groups. PT152-S4-Q23 says the answer must show how a few devices have a large effect.\\r\\n- **They give two or three shapes the answer can take, then sort every choice into them.** PT139-S1-Q15 (trees, birds, or a coincident change) and PT148-S1-Q12 (could not eat, chose not to, ate and the herd grew anyway).\\r\\n- **They use a single test that works on the next question.** \\"Something that changed\\" (PT154-S1-Q6); \\"is this a timeless rule?\\" (PT107-S4-Q19, PT113-S2-Q17); \\"does it separate the two groups?\\" (PT148-S4-Q3).\\r\\n- **On math paradoxes they predict and do the numbers.** PT143-S1-Q19 (averages), PT140-S2-Q7 (cost shares), PT120-S4-Q1 (count vs per-item). Each says why math is the one place to predict.\\r\\n- **On EXCEPT they show the three-and-one split.** PT113-S2-Q17 states it in the D note; PT120-S3-Q15 lists the routes up front; PT101-S3-Q14 uses a fill-in sentence frame for every choice.\\r\\n- **They use the background fact as a weapon against traps.** PT120-S4-Q1 (A and D contradict \\"big films gross 2-3x cost\\"), PT140-S1-Q20 (\\"any answer about wool sales is doomed\\"), PT148-S1-Q12 (it is an island).\\r\\n- **They separate \\"didn\'t decrease\\" from \\"increased\\".** PT139-S1-Q15 on A and B; PT152-S4-Q23 on apprehension rate vs theft rate. If Patricia gets a bad case of heartburn later, she will be grouchy tomorrow morning. So if Patricia eats a heavy, spicy meal tonight, she will be grouchy tomorrow morning. Explanation: Pattern with arrows: P1: X → Y P2: Y → Z Conclusion: X → Z Where X = Patricia eats a heavy, spicy meal; Y = Pat\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Intro Fallacy ID (AnswerKey+explanation).pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Assumption/Notes/Intro Fallacy ID (AnswerKey+explanation).pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-23T01:25:59+00:00  **Words:** 588  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nAnswer Key: Explanations Introduction This answer key provides explanations for the fallacies present in the LSAT worksheet examples. Each fallacy is identified, and the conclusion and counter for each example are detailed. Use this key to understand why each argument is flawed and how to recognize similar fallacies in other LSAT questions. Common Mistakes When identifying fallacies, students often: Confuse different types of fallacies, such as mistaking a Value Assumption for a Sleight of Hand \\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Flaw WS Answer Key  .pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Flaw/Notes/Flaw WS Answer Key  .pdf  **Modified:** 2025-07-18T02:45:30.038000+00:00  **Words:** 584  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nLSAT Logical Reasoning: Flaw Recognition Worksheet - Answer Key Instructions: Each of the following questions presents a ﬂawed argument or a set of answer choices that commonly appear in LSAT Flaw questions. Your task is to identify any logical fallacies/ﬂaws in each question or answer choice. Question 1 Identify the logical fallacy/ﬂaw in the argument below. The latest Connolly Report suggests that Ocksenfrey prepackaged meals are virtually devoid of nutritional value. But the Connolly Report i\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n---\\r\\ntype: teaching-notes\\r\\nsubject: Role answer key Flaw Description Matching (Answer Key)\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-17\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# \\"Role answer key\\" — Flaw Description Matching (Answer Key)\\r\\n\\r\\n> Despite the filename, the actual content is a **flaw-description matching drill**: each item gives an abstract flaw description, then two candidate arguments (A vs. B), and asks which one fits. This is the same skill set tested by Famous Flaws / Method-of-Reasoning Flaw answer choices, not Role.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 1\\r\\n**Description:** Infers that a practice is undesirable solely because it results in negative consequences that are difficult to quantify.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Argument A:** Implementing a new training program in the workplace has uncertain costs, but it may lead to improved employee performance and satisfaction. Therefore, it should not be considered a priority.\\r\\n- **Argument B:** Noise pollution from urban construction projects affects psychological well-being, yet its impact is hard to measure accurately. For this reason, these projects should be limited.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument B — concludes a practice should be limited solely due to hard-to-measure negative impacts.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 2\\r\\n**Description:** Discounts a viewpoint based on the perceived improbability of the conditions being met rather than on evidence negating the viewpoint.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** The theory that the universe is teeming with undiscovered life is dismissed due to the sheer vastness of space and slim chance of such life making contact with Earth.\\r\\n- **B:** The belief in ancient aliens is often refuted through historical records and ongoing scientific research that find no credible artifacts or evidence to support such claims.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — rejects the theory solely due to perceived improbability, not evidence disproving it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 3\\r\\n**Description:** Reasons that because a certain phenomenon leads to a known positive outcome, it must be actively pursued despite unknown variables.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** Investing in space exploration can result in technological advances and inspire generations, so it should be pursued aggressively even though the financial risks are unpredictable.\\r\\n- **B:** Introducing universal basic income could alleviate poverty, yet the economic implications remain uncertain, so careful assessments are necessary before any implementation.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — promotes aggressive pursuit despite unknown risks, based on known benefits.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 4\\r\\n**Description:** Relies on an analogy between two things that are not relevantly similar.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** Anthropologists argue that cultures change much like languages do. Since languages evolve by adopting new expressions and phrases, new cultural norms can be integrated similarly over time.\\r\\n- **B:** Some claim that managing a country is like driving a car; both require adjustment to conditions. However, the complexities of geopolitics transcend the straightforward mechanics of driving.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument B — draws an analogy between unrelated domains (geopolitics vs. driving).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 5\\r\\n**Description:** Derives its conclusion based solely on an event that occurred once, assuming it will happen again in the same manner.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** In the 1800s, a major city overcame economic recession by focusing on industrial growth. Therefore, any city facing a recession today should also turn to industrial development as the solution.\\r\\n- **B:** Because a town hosted a successful annual fair last year, it\'s expected that the same fair will experience success each year.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument B — assumes future success based on a single prior occurrence.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 6\\r\\n**Description:** Improperly shifts the burden of proof by assuming that a claim is true unless it is proven false.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** Critics of the new diet fad say it is unhealthy. Yet, there is minimal research showing definitive harm, so it must be safe for consumption.\\r\\n- **B:** A hypothesis has been proposed that energy drinks increase concentration. Until research proves otherwise, it should be assumed true.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — assumes safety until harm is proven, shifting the burden of proof.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 7\\r\\n**Description:** Confuses necessary conditions with sufficient conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** To graduate, you must complete a thesis. Completing your thesis is enough to ensure graduation.\\r\\n- **B:** She believes that to be healthy, one must exercise. Thus, exercising guarantees health.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — treats a necessary requirement (thesis) as if it guarantees graduation.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 8\\r\\n**Description:** Bases its generalization on a sample that is known to be biased.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** A survey at the local yoga studio found 95% of participants believe yoga is the best form of exercise. Therefore, yoga must be universally considered the best exercise.\\r\\n- **B:** By only interviewing tech company CEOs, the study concludes that all industries are rapidly advancing in AI technology.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — generalizes from a biased sample (yoga enthusiasts).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 9\\r\\n**Description:** Fails to recognize that evidence for the occurrence of one event is not enough to conclude a causation with another.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** Ice cream sales spike in summer, and more people swim during this time. Thus, eating ice cream causes people to swim.\\r\\n- **B:** As daylight hours increase in spring, people tend to spend more time outdoors, hence proving long days lead to more outdoor activities.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — mistakes correlation (ice cream sales and swimming) for causation.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Question 10\\r\\n**Description:** Presents a potentially false dilemma, suggesting there are only two possible choices.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **A:** We must either increase taxes or face economic collapse; there is no other option.\\r\\n- **B:** The city can either build a new park or focus on maintaining current recreational areas to improve community well-being.\\r\\n\\r\\n✅ **Correct Answer:** Argument A — presents only two options while ignoring other possible solutions.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n*Source: Google Doc `1scelLetmAEI4quO4_fH7DUXEdl8DYzhY-MEJe7TmXGA`. Categorized as **Flaw/Famous Flaws** despite the filename — the content is matching abstract flaw descriptions to arguments, which is the famous-flaws/parallel-flaw skill, not Role. Filed here so it doesn\'t pollute the Role folder.*\\r\\n"}'}]

The [[../golden-set|golden set]] (51) is the short list of the very best; this library is the deep bench, 10 to 20 per question type, spread across difficulty and across each type\'s sub-patterns (each famous flaw on Flaw, conditional vs story-style on Parallel, and so on). Most picks are new; golden picks that turned up are marked.\\r\\n\\r\\n**How it was built.** `system/scripts/lr-explanations/score_corpus.py` scored all 3,640 complete explanations (Evaluate depth, a Goal that names the answer, verdict-only notes, explicit tests, examples, stated rules, correct-answer note length, whether the most popular trap got the most attention; penalties for the em-dash register and for mocking the question). The top 25 to 40 per type were then read in full, every choice note checked against the printed answer text, and scored on the golden-set rubric (P prediction, X precision, E eliminations, T transfer, C clarity, L learner value; /12). Reading was split across 9 parallel readers; each file is one reader\'s judgment, so treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.\\r\\n\\r\\n**How to use it.** When writing a missing explanation, find its type here, read the 2 or 3 picks that share its sub-pattern, and follow the \\"What the best ones do\\" section for that type. `system/scripts/lr-explanations/neighbors.py` finds the closest picks by features and stimulus shape; `show.py SLOT` prints one in full. Joshua\'s cram sheet and LR guideline PDFs (paths in [[../golden-set]]) name the moves and traps.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Type | Models | File | Notes |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Flaw | 20 | [[Flaw]] | Leans on flaws the golden set lacks: ad hominem, sampling, anti-causal, percent vs number, aggregate vs subgroup, best vs only, false choice |\\r\\n| Must Be True / Most Supported | 20 | [[MBT-MBF]] | Conditional chains, Most + conditional, weighted averages, pivots, logical completion |\\r\\n| Must Be False | 17 | [[MBT-MBF]] | Only 8 are truly top-tier; the type has 42 complete explanations in total |\\r\\n| Necessary Assumption | 20 | [[NA]] | EXCEPT is thin: one usable model (PT103-S2-Q22, 8/12) |\\r\\n| Sufficient Assumption | 20 | [[Strengthen-SA]] | Includes PT129-S2-Q7, a clean Refute-a-Conditional model the golden set lacked |\\r\\n| Strengthen | 20 | [[Strengthen-SA]] | Plausibility boosters, ruled-out alternatives, plans, EXCEPT |\\r\\n| Weaken | 20 | [[Weaken-Evaluate]] | Third factor, reverse cause, base rate, analogies, term shifts, EXCEPT |\\r\\n| Evaluate | 17 | [[Weaken-Evaluate]] | About 12 are true models; the type has 25 complete explanations |\\r\\n| Paradox | 20 | [[Paradox-Method]] | Difference-makers, math, plan-didn\'t-work, EXCEPT |\\r\\n| Method | 20 | [[Paradox-Method]] | Includes three Method on Plans / Recommendations models the golden set lacked |\\r\\n| Principle-Strengthen | 20 | [[Principle]] | Principles in answers and in the stimulus, weighing tradeoffs |\\r\\n| Principle-Conform | 20 | [[Principle]] | Stimulus principles as checklists, implicit principles as slogans |\\r\\n| Main Conclusion | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Only 5 are difficulty 1-2; more easy models would need a second search |\\r\\n| Role | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Concessions, opposing views, intermediate-conclusion chains |\\r\\n| Agree / Disagree | 20 | [[MC-Role-Agree]] | Explicit and assumption-level clashes, EXCEPT, Agree stems |\\r\\n| Parallel | 20 | [[Parallel]] | No difficulty-1 pick besides the golden PT154-S1-Q10 |\\r\\n| Parallel Flaw | 20 | [[Parallel]] | Each famous flaw; no clean Unproven vs Proven False model |\\r\\n\\r\\n**Do not copy from a pick without reading its Weakness column.** Several picks carry a slip, a political example or a flattened diagram that has to be fixed in the new writing, not reproduced. Every defect found, picked or not, is in [[../fixes/model-library-defects]].\\r\\n\\r\\n**Recurring moves across all types** (each file has the type-specific version):\\r\\n- The Evaluate step argues with the argument before the choices (\\"Given that ... how could we argue ...\\"), with worked numbers or one everyday analogy.\\r\\n- The Goal names the answer\'s content, often in two acceptable shapes (formal and conversational, or linking and defender).\\r\\n- Each choice note runs the test its type or wording calls for, and says so in the first line.\\r\\n- The most popular wrong answer gets the longest note, and it says why it tempts before why it fails.\\r\\n- Wrong answers are often rewritten into the version that would have been right.\\r\\n- Recurring rules are stated as rules, so they transfer to the next question.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 7.3 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"5a3e79","wall_time_seconds":0.512664,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":922,"output":"import json,sys,collections\\r\\nimport sys; sys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding=\\"utf-8\\")\\r\\nDB=\'C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/\'\\r\\nL=lambda t: json.load(open(DB+t+\'.json\',encoding=\'utf-8\'))\\r\\nqs=L(\'lr_questions\'); ex=L(\'lr_explanations\'); st=L(\'lr_explanation_steps\'); nt=L(\'lr_explanation_choice_notes\')\\r\\nQ={f\\"PT{q[\'preptest\']}-S{q[\'section\']}-Q{q[\'question_number\']}\\":q for q in qs}\\r\\nE=collections.defaultdict(list)\\r\\nfor e in ex:\\r\\n  if e[\'style\'] in(\'single\',\'standard\'): E[e[\'question_id\']].append(e[\'id\'])\\r\\nS=collections.defaultdict(list); N=collections.defaultdict(dict)\\r\\nfor s in st: S[s[\'explanation_id\']].append(s)\\r\\nfor n in nt: N[n[\'explanation_id\']][n[\'letter\']]=n[\'body\']\\r\\nfor slot in sys.argv[1:]:\\r\\n  q=Q[slot]; print(\'=====\',slot,q[\'question_type\'],\'key\',q[\'correct_choice\']); print(\'STIM:\',q[\'stimulus\']); print(\'STEM:\',q[\'prompt\'])\\r\\n  for eid in E[q[\'id\']]:\\r\\n    for s in sorted(S[eid],key=lambda s:s[\'position\']): print(f\\"## {s[\'label\']}\\\\n{s[\'body\']}\\")\\r\\n    for k in sorted(N[eid]): print(f\\"-- {k}: {N[eid][k]}\\")\\r\\n### What the best Method explanations do\\r\\n\\r\\n- **They name the move before the choices, from a fixed menu.** Most strong ones list the eight moves (analogy, counterexample, implications, ruling out, alternate explanation, overlooked consideration, dubious assumption, definition/distinction) and pick one. PT103-S1-Q18, PT107-S1-Q12, PT139-S1-Q23.\\r\\n- **They split every answer into its conclusion half and evidence half.** \\"Argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion, Y the evidence\\", then check both. PT118-S4-Q12, PT137-S4-Q12, PT140-S1-Q9.\\r\\n- **They state the second-speaker rule and use it.** The respondent almost never denies the first speaker\'s evidence. PT102-S3-Q11, PT111-S4-Q25, PT118-S1-Q4, PT108-S3-Q13.\\r\\n- **They teach strength words in abstract answers.** \\"Unwarranted\\" is not \\"false\\" (PT139-S1-Q23); \\"inconsistent\\" and \\"self-contradictory\\" are almost never right (PT151-S4-Q21, PT120-S3-Q20); \\"while effective\\" sinks an otherwise perfect answer (PT102-S4-Q19).\\r\\n- **They draw the line between neighbouring moves with a worked contrast.** Analogy vs counterexample with the same story (PT151-S4-Q21); common cause vs another cause (PT119-S2-Q17); distinction vs analogy (PT102-S3-Q11).\\r\\n- **They rewrite a wrong answer into the version that would have been right.** PT137-S4-Q12 (B fixed), PT133-S3-Q19 (A \\"flip its frown\\"). This shows exactly which half failed.\\r\\n- **They convert between Method and the Assumption family.** An objection is an assumption the author made that the objection is false (PT111-S4-Q25); \\"implying\\" in an answer is safe because any objection implies a failure to consider (PT119-S2-Q17).\\r\\n- **They make abstract or dense stimuli concrete first.** PT120-S3-Q20 (Mother\'s Day flowers), PT137-S2-Q12 (a 2x2 grid of trials).\\r\\n\\r\\n### What the weaker Method candidates got wrong\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Mocking the key or the argument.** PT148-S1-Q20 (\\"so, so dumb\\"), PT138-S3-Q20 (\\"real crappy argument\\", \\"This correct answer is miserable\\"), PT152-S1-Q24 (\\"random-ass\\"). The analysis in PT138-S3-Q20 is good, but the tone disqualifies it as a model.\\r\\n- **Uncertainty left on the page.** PT152-S2-Q20 spends its longest note on B (28%) saying \\"this is a tough answer to disprove, for me\\" and \\"my theory is\\". Honest, but the student leaves unsure why B is wrong.\\r\\n- **The top trap gets one line.** PT155-S2-Q23 (A at 20% gets one sentence), PT118-S1-Q7 (D at 19%), PT152-S1-Q24 (D at 23%).\\r\\n- **Goal predicts the wrong move and the notes never reconcile it.** PT136-S2-Q25 predicts \\"ruling out alternatives\\" and has C and D pasted.\\r\\n- **Wrong family.** About a fifth of the Method shortlist is another type (list below).\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 9.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"ab91f1","wall_time_seconds":2.0826955,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2728,"output":"\\r\\n===== PT103-S1-Q18 Method of Reasoning key C\\r\\nSTIM: Politician: A government that taxes incomes at a rate of 100 percent will generate no revenue because all economic activity will cease. So it follows that the lower the rate of income tax, the more revenue the government will generate by that tax.\\r\\n\\r\\nEconomist: Your conclusion cannot be correct, since it would mean that an income tax of 0 percent would generate the maximum revenue.\\r\\nSTEM: Which one of the following argumentative strategies is used by the economist in responding to the politician?\\r\\n## Politician\'s Conclusion (So it follows)\\r\\nThe lower the rate of income tax, the more revenue the government will generate by that tax.\\r\\n## Politician\'s Evidence\\r\\nA government taxing at a 100% rate will generate no revenue because all economic activity will cease.\\r\\n## Economist\'s Conclusion\\r\\nYour conclusion can\'t be correct.\\r\\n*rephrase:*  It is not the case that the lower the income tax rate, the more revenue the government will generate.\\r\\n## Economist\'s Evidence (since)\\r\\nAccording to your rule, an income tax of 0% would generate the maximum revenue.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nMethod question tend to test this set of 8 moves, some of which overlap:\\r\\n- present an analogy\\r\\n- (counter)example to a general claim\\r\\n- rule out alternatives\\r\\n- make a definition / distinction\\r\\n       (these relate especially to rebuttals)\\r\\n- raise an overlooked consideration\\r\\n- call out a faulty assumption\\r\\n- show bad implications of P1\'s logic\\r\\n- suggest an alternate explanation\\r\\n  The economist is clearly rebutting the politician, and she seems to be showing the bad implications of the politician\'s logic.\\r\\n  She\'s saying, \\"The logic of your conclusion implies Y, and Y is wrong/absurd.\\" as a way to show that the conclusion is wrong.\\r\\n  The politician considered an extreme case (maximum income tax rate of 100% would generate no revenue). The economist considers the opposite extreme:  minimum income tax rate of 0% would also generate no revenue.\\r\\n  This allows her to prove that \\"the **lower** the rate, the **higher** the revenue\\" is too extreme a formulation. When you take it to its logical extreme, you get the absurd idea that 0% tax rate would generate the most tax revenue.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for anything that matches, but it will probably sound like it\'s saying the economist \\"shows how the politician\'s conclusion commits him to an absurd implication\\".\\r\\n-- A: The economist doesn\'t state any general principles. She just mentions one specific mathematical example of the general principle stated in the politician\'s conclusion.\\r\\n-- B: This answer is saying that the economist pointed to society\'s where they adopted the politician\'s advice, lowered the tax rate, and found a disappointing uptick in the tax revenue generated by that.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe economist, of course, presents nothing resembling that. She just points out that by the logic of the politician\'s rule, the time when you would get the most tax revenue is when you taxed 0% of people\'s income.\\r\\n-- C: This is giving us the \\"wrong/absurd implications\\" answer we were looking for. The economist argues that the politician\'s principle leads to an absurdly false conclusion, because she points out that by the logic of the politician\'s rule, the time when you would get the most tax revenue is when you taxed 0% of people\'s income.\\r\\n\\r\\nHow could you get *any* revenue if you were taxing 0% of people\'s income?\\r\\n\\r\\nThe only weird part about this answer is the whole \\"if applied in the limiting case\\". What is a *limiting case*?\\r\\n\\r\\nNot sure, but it seems to be legal mumbo jumbo for \\"pushing a rule to its limit\\", since what the economist is doing is looking at the lower extreme of the politician\'s rule\\r\\n-- D: This doesn\'t match the evidence at all. The economist says \\"that would mean an income tax of 0 percent would generate the max revenue\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nThat is a dry, neutral, mathematical claim.\\r\\n\\r\\nAccording to this answer, the economist\'s premise was a direct accusation that the politician doesn\'t understand economics.\\r\\n\\r\\nSure, the economist is disagreeing with the politician\'s conclusion, which is economically related, so the economist definitely thinks the politician is wrong about at least one idea within economics.\\r\\n\\r\\nBut all that is off the page. We can infer that the economist would agree that the politician misunderstands the economic relationship between tax rates and tax revenue at least somewhat.\\r\\n\\r\\nBut there is no explicit premise where the economist says, \\"I don\'t think you know what you\'er talking about\\".\\r\\n-- E: Almost every Method question with 2-Speakers has this trap answer, in which they accuse the 2nd speaker of doing the incredibly-rare move of questioning whether a premise is true.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe economist doesn\'t dispute the politician\'s first sentence (his premise). She just takes issue with his conclusion.\\r\\n===== PT139-S1-Q23 Method of Reasoning key A\\r\\nSTIM: Anthropologist: Every human culture has taboos against eating certain animals. Some researchers have argued that such taboos originated solely for practical reasons, pointing out, for example, that in many cultures it is taboo to eat domestic animals that provide labor and that are therefore worth more alive than dead. But that conclusion is unwarranted; taboos against eating certain animals might instead have arisen for symbolic, ritualistic reasons, and the presence of the taboos might then have led people to find other uses for those animals.\\r\\nSTEM: In the argument, the anthropologist\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nThe conclusion that food taboos originated for practical reasons (certain animals are worth more alive than dead) is unwarranted.\\r\\n## Evidence\\r\\nFood taboos might have originated for symbolic, ritualistic reasons, and then since people weren\'t allowed to eat these animals, they then figured out practical uses for them.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nThis feels like how we often analyze the Curious Fact / Explanation arguments we see in Strengthen and Weaken. There was a curious phenomenon: why does every human culture have some taboos against eating certain animals? One possible explanation (posed by some researchers) is that humans invent taboos for the animals they want to keep alive for practical reasons. Our author says, \\"Hey you can\'t be so sure of that explanation. After all, you could seemingly explain that a different way (humans invent taboos for religious reasons, and then since no one can kill these animals they invent practical uses for them).\\"\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for something like, argues that one possible explanation for a phenomenon is unwarranted on the grounds that an alternate explanation for that phenomenon exists.\\r\\n-- A: When we see\\r\\nDoes THIS by doing THAT\\r\\nThe \\"by\\" shows us the evidence, the other part is the conclusion.\\r\\nA lawyer proves GUILT by producing evidence.\\r\\nDoes this answer match the conclusion and evidence?\\r\\nDid the author\'s conclusion call an explanation of a phenomenon into question?\\r\\n- Yes. She said that explaining the phenomenon of food taboos by saying that they are for practical reasons is an unwarranted conclusion.\\r\\nDid the evidence point out that the cited evidence is compatible with an alternative explanation?\\r\\n- Yes. She said, \\"Taboos might instead have arisen for [other] reasons\\".\\r\\nSome of the cited evidence for \\"food taboos exist for practical reasons\\" is that there are taboos against animals that provide labor, i.e. that are practically useful.\\r\\nOur author explains that \\"the taboos may have come first, (for religious reasons), and then people found uses for those animals second\\".\\r\\n-- B: Does the conclusion establish that an explanation is false?\\r\\nNo, she only says that it\'s unwarranted. That means \\"you haven\'t convinced me you\'re right yet\\". It doesn\'t mean, \\"I\'m convinced you\'re wrong\\".\\r\\nWe can stop reading this answer at this point.\\r\\nThe second half almost works. \\"Demonstrating\\" is too strong. Raising an alternate explanation possibility is softer than \\"demonstrating\\".\\r\\nBut otherwise, the second half is probably fair. Any author saying, \\"How do you know it\'s X? Couldn\'t it be Y?\\" apparently believes that the evidence for X was inadequate.\\r\\n-- C: Does the author reject the reasoning used to justify a hypothesis?\\r\\nSure. Our author is not persuaded (conclusion is unwarranted) by the researchers\' hypothesis that the origins of food taboos was practical concerns.\\r\\nIs our author\'s evidence that there is another, more plausible hypothesis?\\r\\nNo, our author doesn\'t indicate that it\'s more plausible. If an alternate hypothesis is even equally plausible, it serves the same effect: it shows that someone was hasty in concluding a certain causal story, when other possible causal stories haven\'t been ruled out.\\r\\nOur author brings up her alternate explanation by saying \\"taboos might instead have arisen for symbolic reasons\\".\\r\\n-- D: Does our author\'s conclusion argue in support of one explanation?\\r\\nNo, this is like (C) calling our author\'s alternate explanation \\"the more plausible\\" option. The author is agnostic about which causal story is correct.\\r\\nShe is concluding that \\"an inference is unwarranted\\". Unwarranted doesn\'t mean wrong; it just means too hastily drawn, unproven.\\r\\nShe also doesn\'t cite evidence that\'s incompatible (i.e. contradictory) to the researchers\' evidence. In fact, as the correct answer reminds us, her alternate explanation is completely compatible with the author\'s evidence about food taboo animals often being useful animals used for labor.\\r\\n-- E: There is a hypothesis about the sequence of events involved in the origins of the food taboo phenomenon:\\r\\nResearchers hypothesis\\r\\npeople realize horses are useful. people invent a food taboos for horses, because they\'re worth more alive than dead.\\r\\nThe author talks about a different sequence, sort of:\\r\\npeople invent a food taboo for horses, for symbolic and ritualistic reasons, and then people realize horses (which they can\'t kill because of this taboo) are useful.\\r\\nThose aren\'t quite the same events in reverse. The useful / taboo relationship is switched, but the author\'s explanation involves a particular event (\\"taboo arose for symbolic reasons\\") that isn\'t in the original story.\\r\\nPerhaps the easiest way to get rid of this without thinking too hard is again to just understand the noncommittal strength of arguing \\"that conclusion is unwarranted\\".\\r\\nAll a person needs to do to win that conversation is create some doubt about the original conclusion. The author isn\'t personally invested in this alternative hypothesis; she\'s just bringing it up as a possibility. We can say that she presents the possibility that some of those events were in a different sequence, but saying that she argues they were in a different sequence is ascribing to her a conclusion that is confidently choosing one possibility.\\r\\nName\\r\\n----\\r\\nlr_answer_choices.json\\r\\nlr_explanation_choice_notes.json\\r\\nlr_explanation_steps.json\\r\\nlr_explanations.json\\r\\nlr_questions.json\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 5.6 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"3320ed","wall_time_seconds":0.7246691,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1101,"output":"===== PT118-S4-Q12 Method of Reasoning key A\\r\\nSTIM: Columnist: If you received an unsigned letter, you would likely have some doubts about the truth of its contents. But news stories often include statements from anonymous sources, and these are usually quoted with the utmost respect. It makes sense to be skeptical of these sources, for, as in the case of the writer of an unsigned letter, their anonymity makes it possible for them to plant inaccurate or slanted statements without ever having to answer for them.\\r\\nSTEM: The columnist\'s argument proceeds by\\r\\n## Conclusion\\r\\nIt makes sense to be skeptical of anonymous sources in news stories.\\r\\n## Evidence (for)\\r\\nIf you received an unsigned letter, you would likely have some doubts about the truth of its contents.\\r\\nSimilarly, the anonymity of an unnamed source makes it possible for them to plant inaccurate or slanted statements without ever having to answer for them.\\r\\n## Evaluate\\r\\nMethod questions want us to characterize the reasoning move or type of evidence used, and the correct answers usually pull from this list: 1. make/ break an analogy 2. show faulty implications of logic / plan 3. eliminate / suggest an alternative\\r\\nThis argument definitely makes an analogy (two specific cases with some similar general principle).\\r\\nIn both the case of the unsigned letter and the anonymous source, it makes sense to be skeptical about the accuracy of what you hear.\\r\\n## Goal\\r\\nLook for something like, \\"supports the idea that we should be skeptical of anonymous sources in news stories by presenting an analogy about unsigned letters\\".\\r\\n-- A: The \\"analogous situation\\" certainly scratches our itch. Can we match up the rest?\\r\\n\\r\\nThe author points out that a **skeptical** attitude would presumably ( **likely** ) be adopted in the case of [receiving an unsigned letter], in order to support the claim that a similar attitude ( **skepticism** ) would be justified in an analogous situation [reading a news story with anonymous sources].\\r\\n-- B: We could potentially match up everything leading up to the comma.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe attitude commonly adopted in anonymous letters is \\"skeptical\\" of the contents.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe attitude commonly adopted when quoting anonymous sources is an article is \\"utmost respect\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nBut the second half of this is saying that the author argues that treating anonymous comments with utmost respect **is better justified** than treating them with skepticism, and the conclusion is saying the opposite of that.\\r\\n-- C: Since this answer takes the form: inferring X on the grounds that Y we need to see if X matches the conclusion and Y matches the evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\nDoes the author conclude that \\"an attitude would be justified in all situations of a given type\\"? Sure, we could make that work. She concludes that a skeptical attitude would be justified in all situations in which a news story quotes an anonymous source.\\r\\n\\r\\nOkay, does the evidence talk about a hypothetical situation in which a news story quotes an anonymous source?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo, it talks about a hypothetical situation of a **different** (though analogous) **type** -- if you receive an unsigned letter.\\r\\n-- D: Since this answer takes the form: doing X by doing Y we need to see if X matches the conclusion and Y matches the evidence.\\r\\n\\r\\nDoes the author\'s conclusion \\"call into question a certain type of evidence\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nSure, we could make that work. She concludes that a skeptical attitude would be justified in all situations in which a news story quotes an anonymous source, so she\'s calling into question the evidence of an anonymous source.\\r\\n\\r\\nOkay, does the evidence talk about an analogous type of evidence that is usually false?\\r\\n\\r\\nNo. It talks about an analogous type of evidence (unsigned letters), but it doesn\'t say that they are usually false. It just says that people would likely have doubts about their truth.\\r\\n-- E: Since this answer takes the form: doing X and concluding for this reason Y we would want to see if X matches the evidence and Y matches the conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\nDoes the author conclude that \\"certain information is likely to be false\\"?\\r\\n\\r\\nNope. She just says being skeptical of information backed up by anonymous sources is justified / makes sense.\\r\\n\\r\\nSaying that it makes sense to be skeptical of information like X doesn\'t mean that you\'re saying information like X is usually false, just that there\'s a higher than normal risk that it could be false.\\r\\n"}'}]

## assistant

Keep M4–M7, but give each a distinct operation and source-grounded worked example.

| Module | Prerequisite | Distinct task | Source-grounded teaching |
|---|---|---|---|
| M4 Match | Student can state an answer’s requirements | Point to actual stimulus language satisfying every requirement | Revisit role-source exercise 4: celebrities’ smiles, happiness claim, David’s rebuttal |
| M5 Method | Student distinguishes conclusion, evidence and opponent | Describe what the author does with the evidence | PT103-S1-Q18: economist tests the politician’s lower-tax claim at 0% |
| M6 Traps | Student can map the described move | Identify the exact word or relation the stimulus does not justify | PT139-S1-Q23: food taboos, practical versus symbolic explanations |
| M7 Integration | Student can decode, map and reject mismatches | Work through a fresh full question with all five choices | PT118-S4-Q12: unsigned letters and anonymous news sources |

**M4 blueprint:** Start with the same exact role-source exercise 4 used earlier, explicitly called a revisit. Earlier the student predicted the required features and invented an argument. Now the real source argument supplies the content. Map:

- Observation: celebrities smiling in front of the camera.
- Opponent’s claim: celebrities are happiest.
- Insufficient support: David says smiling does not mean anything and may be a performance.
- Rejection of the opponent’s claim: “That is not true.”

Each piece needs an actual quotation or stated relationship. Finish with the difference between understanding the choice and finding its description in an actual argument.

Grounding: [matching source](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/raw/sources/lsat-u/curriculum/Abstract/_source/03-art-of-matching-keywords.md:18), source role-choice HTML exercise 4. Current M4 drills primarily test ordinary role selection, so they only partially train this finer mapping operation.

**M5 blueprint:** Use PT103-S1-Q18 as the substantive lecture. The politician extrapolates from 100% tax yielding zero revenue to “lower tax means more revenue.” The economist applies that principle at the other extreme: 0% taxation would supposedly maximize revenue. The keyed choice describes an absurd implication in the limiting case. Teach the move by showing its components, then contrast it with attacking a premise, attacking the person and reporting actual failed policy trials.

Do not force learners to memorize a thin list of 17 names first. Introduce recurring moves through actual arguments, then use the library as reference. [Model synthesis](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/models/Paradox-Method.md:101) documents eight recurring moves and worked contrasts. The original 17-method source remains missing from the bounded archive inventory. Current quiz items mp4–mp6 present definitions/instructions as “arguments”; replace those with actual sourced stimuli.

**M6 blueprint:** Use PT139-S1-Q23. The anthropologist offers an alternative explanation compatible with the observed evidence. The conclusion is **unwarranted**, not established false. Compare the actual choices:

- B: “establishes … false” exceeds what the author says.
- C: “more plausible” is not established.
- D: “incompatible” misdescribes compatible evidence.
- E: “argues … occurred” overstates “might.”

Teach the actual failed requirement before assigning a trap category. Categories can summarize the error afterward. This has stronger transfer than memorizing four labels.

**M7 blueprint:** PT118-S4-Q12 is fresh relative to the 15 role-choice exercises. Present the complete stimulus, stem and five actual choices. The author analogizes skepticism toward an unsigned letter to skepticism toward anonymous news sources.

For keyed A, map the skeptical attitude, hypothetical unsigned letter, analogous anonymous-source situation and conclusion. Wrong B reverses the justified attitude, C substitutes the same situation type for an analogous one, D claims “usually false,” and E equates justified skepticism with information probably being false. Require the student’s attempt before explanations.

Replace M7’s seven duplicated translations with full integration. Do not call repeated choices “fresh.”

All three PT items are locally available in the preserved **2026-08-03 database snapshot**, routed from [model index](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/models/index.md:12):

- [lr_questions.json](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/lr_questions.json): exact stimulus, prompt and key.
- [lr_answer_choices.json](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db/lr_answer_choices.json): exact five choices.
- [show.py](C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/system/scripts/lr-explanations/show.py:1): targeted explanation retrieval.
- Slot identifiers: `PT103-S1-Q18`, `PT139-S1-Q23`, `PT118-S4-Q12`.

These are captured source records, not freshly verified live question-bank state. The content worker has the selections and retrieval details.

Avoid the corpus “Role answer key (flaw matching)” as a grading source: Q6 and Q7 each have two fitting arguments, and Q4’s keyed argument criticizes an analogy instead of relying on it. Preserve those conflicts rather than copying their keys into new practice.

b) => a.completedAt.localeCompare(b.completedAt)),\\r\\n        );\\r\\n        const lastAttemptCorrect = drillStats.every(\\r\\n          (attempts) => attempts.length > 0 && attempts[attempts.length - 1].correct,\\r\\n        );\\r\\n        const drillCorrect = drillStats.reduce(\\r\\n          (n, attempts) =>\\r\\n            n + (attempts.length > 0 && attempts[attempts.length - 1].correct ? 1 : 0),\\r\\n          0,\\r\\n        );\\r\\n        const aced = quizPerfect && lastAttemptCorrect;\\r\\n        const candidate: ModuleProgressRow = {\\r\\n          ...existing,\\r\\n          drillComplete: true,\\r\\n          drillCorrect,\\r\\n          drillTotal: mod.drillIds.length,\\r\\n          aced,\\r\\n          completedAt: new Date().toISOString(),\\r\\n        };\\r\\n        const merged: ModuleProgressRow = {\\r\\n          ...candidate,\\r\\n          masteryLevel: computeMastery(candidate),\\r\\n        };\\r\\n        const nextProgress = { ...prev.moduleProgress, [moduleId]: merged };\\r\\n        const nextAceBadges = aced && !prev.aceBadges.includes(moduleId)\\r\\n          ? [...prev.aceBadges, moduleId]\\r\\n"}'}]

But that conclusion is unwarranted; taboos against eating certain animals might instead have arisen for symbolic, ritualistic reasons, and the presence of the taboos might then have led people to find other uses for those animals.\\",\\r\\n    \\"question\\": \\"In the argument, the anthropologist\\",\\r\\n    \\"options\\": [\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"calls an explanation of a phenomenon into question by pointing out that observations cited as evidence supporting it are also compatible with an alternative explanation of the phenomenon\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"The same useful-animal observation can fit symbolic taboos arising first.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"establishes that an explanation of a phenomenon is false by demonstrating that the evidence that had been cited in support of that explanation was inadequate\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"Unwarranted does not establish false.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"rejects the reasoning used to justify a hypothesis about the origins of a phenomenon, on the grounds that there exists another, more plausible hypothesis about the origins of that phenomenon\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"The alternative is possible, not established as more plausible.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"argues in support of one explanation of a phenomenon by citing evidence incompatible with a rival explanation\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"The observations are compatible with both explanations.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": \\"describes a hypothesis about the sequence of events involved in the origins of a phenomenon, and then argues that those events occurred in a different sequence\\",\\r\\n        \\"explanation\\": \\"The alternative sequence might have occurred; \\"explanation\\": \\"The reasoning is an analogy supporting a similar attitude, not a universal inference about all situations of a type.\\"\\r\\n      },\\r\\n      {\\r\\n        \\"text\\": anonymous news sources with the same accountability problem.\\",\\r\\n    \\"trapTag\\": \\"wrong_method\\"\\r\\n  }\\r\\n];\\r\\n\\r\\nexport const getAppliedSourceQuestion = (id: string) =>
