[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 7.8 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"18deb6","wall_time_seconds":1.0562068,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4704,"output":"src/topics/abstract\\\\data\\\\appliedSourceQuestions.ts\\nsrc/topics/abstract\\\\components\\\\views\\\\AdvancedChoiceLessons.tsx\\nimport { useState } from \\"react\\";\\r\\nimport { getAppliedSourceQuestion } from \\"../../data/appliedSourceQuestions\\";\\r\\n\\r\\n// Local authored lessons from the top M4-M7 draft in ai-os/brainstorms/\\r\\n// 2026-10-13-abstract-answer-choice-review.md (2026-10-13); pending Joshua review.\\r\\n// M4: Desktop role_question_answer_choices_complete_transcription.html, item 4.\\r\\n// M5-M7: captured 2026-08-03 lr_questions.json/lr_answer_choices.json via the LR model index:\\r\\n// PT103-S1-Q18, PT139-S1-Q23, PT118-S4-Q12. Full exact questions use the shared source data.\\r\\n// Reasoning also grounded in Abstract 03-art-of-matching-keywords, Role recap,\\r\\n// Advanced Parsing Answer Key, and Method and Flaw Strategy Guide. No added scenarios.\\r\\nconst MATCHING_HTML = \\"<h2>A description has to fit this argument</h2>\\\\n<p>An answer choice can sound familiar and still describe the wrong relationship. Now we’ll use an argument that is already written. For every requirement in the choice, we need to point to the part of that argument that fulfills it.</p>\\\\n<p>Read this exchange:</p>\\\\n<p class=\\\\\\"example\\\\\\"><strong>John:</strong> <span class=\\\\\\"underlined\\\\\\">Celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</span>, so they must be the happiest people alive.</p>\\\\n<p class=\\\\\\"example\\\\\\"><strong>David:</strong> That is not true. Many celebrities suffer from depression and substance abuse. Smiling in front of the camera doesn’t mean a thing, they could simply be putting on a show for the audience.</p>\\\\n<p>The statement we are examining is John’s observation that celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera. David is responding to John’s argument. Keep those two speakers separate as we read the choice.</p>\\\\n<p>Here is the answer choice:</p>\\\\n<blockquote>It makes an observation that, according to the argument, is insufficient to justify the claim that the argument concludes is false</blockquote>\\\\n<h2>Find the claim the author rejects</h2>\\\\n<p>The choice says that the argument concludes a claim is false. Which claim?</p>\\\\n<p>John says celebrities must be the happiest people alive. David opens with “That is not true,” then points to depression and substance abuse. So the rejected claim is John’s conclusion about their happiness. The statement under examination is not David’s objection. It is the observation John used to reach the claim David rejects.</p>\\\\n<h2>Find the observation and check whether it is enough</h2>\\\\n<p>John’s reason is the smiles he sees in front of the camera. David says smiling “doesn’t mean a thing” and suggests celebrities could be putting on a show. David is challenging what those smiles establish. They do not justify John’s conclusion, according to David’s argument.</p>\\\\n<p>We can now match the description completely:</p>\\\\n<div class=\\\\\\"overflow-x-auto\\\\\\"><table><thead><tr><th>Requirement in the choice</th><th>Where it appears in the original argument</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>An observation is made</td><td>John: celebrities have the biggest smiles in front of the camera</td></tr><tr><td>The observation is used to justify a claim</td><td>John uses the smiles to conclude celebrities are the happiest people alive</td></tr><tr><td>The author treats the observation as insufficient</td><td>David says the smiles do not establish happiness and could be a performance</td></tr><tr><td>The argument concludes the supported claim is false</td><td>David rejects John’s claim and cites depression and substance abuse</td></tr></tbody></table></div>\\\\n<p><strong>False describes the claim David rejects. It does not describe the observation about the smiles.</strong> That difference matters. If we moved “false” onto the observation, we would be reading a different choice.</p>\\\\n<p>Before accepting a description, say which statement it refers to and point to every relationship it requires. A passage containing all the right topics is not enough. The right statements must be doing the right jobs.</p>\\\\n<p>reread the original exchange and choice. Identify the observation, the claim it supports, and David’s response in separate written lines. Compare each line with the matching table.</p>\\";\\r\\nconst MODULE_5_INTRO = \\"<h2>Follow the rule to the case the economist chooses</h2>\\\\n<p>The argument below is about taxation. That is its subject. The question asks how the economist responds to the politician, so we need to follow what the economist does with the politician’s reasoning.</p>\\";\\r\\nconst MODULE_5_EXPLANATION = \\"<p>Start with the politician’s conclusion: the lower the income-tax rate, the more revenue the tax generates. The politician takes the result at a 100-percent rate and draws a broad relationship between lower rates and higher revenue.</p>\\\\n<p>The economist does not cite a country where a tax cut failed. The economist does not dispute that economic activity would cease at the 100-percent rate. The economist takes the politician’s conclusion to the other end of the range: a rate of zero percent.</p>\\\\n<p>If lowering the rate always raises revenue, a zero-percent rate would produce the maximum. But a tax charged at zero percent collects no income-tax revenue. The economist uses that consequence to challenge the politician’s conclusion.</p>\\\\n<div class=\\\\\\"definition\\\\\\"><p><strong>That is the reasoning move: apply the politician’s rule at its lower limit and show that the resulting conclusion cannot be right.</strong></p></div>\\\\n<h2>Connect that move to the answer’s language</h2>\\\\n<p>Choice C calls the politician’s conclusion a <strong>principle</strong>. Here it is the general relationship the politician claims: lower rate, more revenue. <strong>If applied in the limiting case</strong> refers to applying that relationship at the zero-percent rate. <strong>Leads to an absurdly false conclusion</strong> refers to the claim that a zero-percent income tax would generate maximum revenue.</p>\\\\n<p>Each part names something we can point to. The answer is C.</p>\\\\n<p>Notice how other choices change the action. B describes real results from places that adopted the advice; no such evidence is given. D describes an open attack on the politician’s understanding of economics; the economist instead points to a consequence of the politician’s conclusion. E says a premise is challenged; the response challenges the conclusion drawn from the premise.</p>\\\\n<p>Before selecting a methods label, explain the actual move in ordinary words. Here, “the economist uses a zero-percent tax to show what goes wrong with the politician’s rule” connects the reason to the response. “They disagree about taxes” does not.</p>\\\\n<p>identify the politician’s rule, the case the economist applies it to, and the result that makes the rule untenable. Then match those three parts to C.</p>\\";\\r\\nconst MODULE_6_INTRO = \\"<h2>Keep the author’s degree of commitment</h2>\\\\n<p>A description can correctly identify the topic and still say the author did more than the passage shows. Read this full original question. Pay attention to <strong>unwarranted</strong>, <strong>might instead</strong>, and <strong>might then</strong> in the stimulus.</p>\\";\\r\\nconst MODULE_6_EXPLANATION = \\"<p>The researchers conclude that the taboos originated solely for practical reasons. They point to useful domestic animals that are worth more alive than dead.</p>\\\\n<p>The anthropologist says that conclusion is unwarranted. Why? There is another possible explanation: symbolic or ritualistic taboos could have come first, and people could then have found practical uses for the protected animals.</p>\\\\n<p>The observation about useful animals fits that alternative too. So the anthropologist challenges whether the cited observation settles the origin of the taboo. The argument does not establish which origin actually occurred.</p>\\\\n<h2>Check the correct description completely</h2>\\\\n<p>Choice A says the author <strong>calls an explanation into question</strong>. That fits “that conclusion is unwarranted.” It then says the observations cited as evidence are <strong>also compatible with an alternative explanation</strong>. That fits the possibility that taboos came first and practical uses were found afterward.</p>\\\\n<p>The answer is A. Both parts match. The alternative can account for the observation without contradicting the fact that those animals have practical value.</p>\\\\n<h2>Find the exact point where each nearby choice changes the argument</h2>\\\\n<p>B says the author <strong>establishes ... is false</strong>. The author challenges whether the researchers have established their explanation; the anthropologist does not establish that it is false. “Unwarranted” does not do the same work as “false.”</p>\\\\n<p>C calls the alternative <strong>more plausible</strong>. The author raises it as a possibility. She does not compare the explanations and establish that the alternative is more plausible.</p>\\\\n<p>D describes evidence <strong>incompatible with a rival explanation</strong>. The point is that the same observation can fit another explanation. Compatibility is the reason the original explanation has not been settled.</p>\\\\n<p>E says the author <strong>argues that those events occurred in a different sequence</strong>. The anthropologist presents another sequence with “might.” It does not conclude that sequence actually occurred.</p>\\\\n<p>This is how we diagnose a close answer. Quote the word that adds strength or changes the relationship, then compare it with what the author actually commits to. Do not replace a careful comparison with “it sounds too strong.” Explain what is too strong and why.</p>\\\\n<p>for B through E, identify the exact phrase that does not fit and point to the stimulus wording that settles the mismatch. Then explain how both parts of A fit.</p>\\";\\r\\nconst MODULE_7_INTRO = \\"<h2>Make your own account before opening the explanation</h2>\\\\n<p>Read the original argument and all five choices. Identify the columnist’s conclusion and the reason for it. Explain the author’s move in ordinary language. Then choose a description and show where each part of it appears in the argument.</p>\\";\\r\\nconst MODULE_7_EXPLANATION = \\"<h2>First, follow the argument</h2>\\\\n<p>The conclusion is that it makes sense to be skeptical of anonymous sources in news stories. The columnist compares those sources with the writer of an unsigned letter.</p>\\\\n<p>What connects the cases? In both, anonymity allows inaccurate or slanted statements without the speaker or writer having to answer for them. The columnist uses the doubt we would likely have about an unsigned letter to support skepticism toward anonymous news sources.</p>\\\\n<p>That gives us an account of the move before we rely on a label: <strong>use our likely response to one anonymous source to justify a similar response to another, where the same anonymity problem applies</strong>.</p>\\\\n<h2>Decode the full answer and map every part</h2>\\\\n<p>Answer A describes the move. Break its description into requirements:</p>\\\\n<div class=\\\\\\"overflow-x-auto\\\\\\"><table><thead><tr><th>Part of A</th><th>Match in the original argument</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>A certain attitude would presumably be adopted</td><td>You would likely have doubts about the truth of an unsigned letter</td></tr><tr><td>In one situation</td><td>Receiving an unsigned letter</td></tr><tr><td>To support the claim that a similar attitude would be justified</td><td>The columnist concludes skepticism makes sense</td></tr><tr><td>In an analogous situation</td><td>Reading news stories that include anonymous sources</td></tr></tbody></table></div>\\\\n<p>The final sentence explains why the situations are compared: in each, anonymity allows inaccurate or slanted claims without accountability. The analogy is doing support work, rather than merely mentioning two different kinds of writing.</p>\\\\n<h2>Check the alternatives without changing the conclusion</h2>\\\\n<p>B says the <strong>latter attitude is better justified</strong>. That would defend the respect given to anonymous sources over the doubt directed toward unsigned letters. The conclusion instead supports skepticism toward anonymous sources.</p>\\\\n<p>C describes an inference about <strong>all situations of a given type</strong> from a hypothetical situation <strong>of that type</strong>. That is not the reasoning the columnist gives. The columnist uses the likely doubt about an unsigned letter to support a similar cautious attitude toward anonymous news sources, explaining the feature the cases share. The passage does not establish a universal rule for every situation in a defined category merely from one hypothetical case. Check the whole description of the inference: A captures the comparison and the justified similar attitude. Do not reject C just because the examples have different names; analogous cases can share a broader category. The mismatch is C’s description of a same-type general inference to all situations, rather than the analogy the author actually uses.</p>\\\\n<p>D says the analogous evidence is <strong>usually false</strong>. The columnist says we would likely have doubts about an unsigned letter. Doubts about truth do not establish that such letters are usually false.</p>\\\\n<p>E says the conclusion is that the information is <strong>likely to be false</strong>. The columnist concludes that skepticism makes sense. The possibility of inaccurate or slanted statements supports that cautious attitude; it does not establish the stronger conclusion E states.</p>\\\\n<h2>Explain your choice fully</h2>\\\\n<p>If your answer matched A, check whether you matched every part or only recognized “analogous.” If you chose another answer, identify the exact phrase that changed the attitude, the situation, or the strength of the conclusion. Then revise your written account of the argument.</p>\\\\n<p>You have now used the whole procedure on a fresh full source question: understand the structure, decode the description, list its requirements, match them to the argument, and check each qualification. The same procedure gives you a reason to accept the answer and a precise reason to reject a close alternative.</p>\\\\n<p>This completes the course’s applied sequence. You can return to the fifteen-choice exercise for language practice or to these source questions to practice mapping that language to an argument.</p>\\\\n<p>### Implementation checkpoint for the retained modules, 2026-10-13</p>\\\\n<p>Root accepted the four lessons above for local implementation. Notes/build agents are applying them and verifying the result. Original source introduction and fifteen breakdowns remain exact. The later lessons use one deliberate source-example revisit for M4 and complete sourced questions for M5–M7. The existing Final Set is held because its synthetic questions have defects; it is not treated as accepted source practice or released. No completed build, account acceptance, publication or parity claim is made in this checkpoint. Older course-ending-at-M3 proposals and implementation snapshots below are historical and superseded by Joshua’s explicit keep-Modules-4–7 decision.</p>\\";\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction LessonProse({ html }: { \\"../types\\";\\r\\nimport { sourceExerciseIds } from \\"./grading-status\\";\\r\\nimport { LECTURE_DRILL_IDS, LECTURE_LESSON_IDS } from \\"../data/modules\\";\\r\\n\\r\\nexport interface ProgressBreakdown {\\r\\n  lessons: { done: number; total: number; pct: number };\\r\\n  translator: { done: number; total: number; pct: number };\\r\\n  gauntlet: { done: number; total: number; pct: number };\\r\\n  drills: { done: number; total: number; pct: number };\\r\\n  flashcards: { done: number; total: number; pct: number };\\r\\n  finalSet: { done: number; total: number; pct: number };\\r\\n  overallPct: number;\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nconst WEIGHTS = {\\r\\n  lessons: 0.25,\\r\\n  translator: 0.2,\\r\\n  gauntlet: 0.2,\\r\\n  drills: 0.35,\\r\\n  flashcards: 0,\\r\\n  finalSet: 0,\\r\\n} as const;\\r\\n\\r\\nfunction pct(done: number, total: number): number {\\r\\n  if (total <= 0) return 0;\\r\\n  return Math.min(100, Math.round((done / total) * 100));\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport function computeProgress(state: BootcampState): ProgressBreakdown {\\r\\n  const lessonsTotal = LECTURE_LESSON_IDS.size;\\r\\n  const lessonsDone = state.completedLessons.filter((id) => LECTURE_LESSON_IDS.has(id)).length;\\r\\n\\r\\n  // Only the answer choices a student can actually reach count toward progress.\\r\\n  const translatorIds = new Set(ANSWER_CHOICE_ITEMS.map((i) => i.id));\\r\\n  const translatorTotal = translatorIds.size;\\r\\n  const translatorDone = [...sourceExerciseIds(state.drillAttempts)].filter((id) => translatorIds.has(id)).length;\\r\\n\\r\\n  const gauntletIds = new Set(APPLIED_SOURCE_QUESTIONS.map((item) => item.id));\\r\\n  const gauntletTotal = gauntletIds.size;\\r\\n  const gauntletDone = new Set(\\r\\n    state.completedDrills.filter((id) => gauntletIds.has(id)),\\r\\n  ).size;\\r\\n\\r\\n  const drillIds = new Set(DRILLS.filter((item) => LECTURE_DRILL_IDS.has(item.id) && !gauntletIds.has(item.id)).map((item) => item.id));\\r\\n  const drillsTotal = drillIds.size;\\r\\n  const drillsDone = state.completedDrills.filter((id) => drillIds.has(id)).length;\\r\\n\\r\\n  const flashcardsTotal = FLASHCARDS.length;\\r\\n  const flashcardsDone = Object.values(state.cards).filter((c) => c.status === \\"mastered\\").length;\\r\\n\\r\\n  const finalSetTotal = 0;\\r\\n  const finalSetDone = 0;\\r\\n\\r\\n  const lessonsPct = pct(lessonsDone, lessonsTotal);\\r\\n  const translatorPct = pct(translatorDone, translatorTotal);\\r\\n  const gauntletPct = pct(gauntletDone, gauntletTotal);\\r\\n  const drillsPct = pct(drillsDone, drillsTotal);\\r\\n  const flashcardsPct = pct(flashcardsDone, flashcardsTotal);\\r\\n  const finalSetPct = pct(finalSetDone, finalSetTotal);\\r\\n\\r\\n  const overallPct = Math.round(\\r\\n    lessonsPct * WEIGHTS.lessons +\\r\\n      translatorPct * WEIGHTS.translator +\\r\\n      gauntletPct * WEIGHTS.gauntlet +\\r\\n      drillsPct * WEIGHTS.drills +\\r\\n      flashcardsPct * WEIGHTS.flashcards +\\r\\n      finalSetPct * WEIGHTS.finalSet,\\r\\n  );\\r\\n\\r\\n  return {\\r\\n    lessons: { done: lessonsDone, total: lessonsTotal, pct: lessonsPct },\\r\\n    translator: { done: translatorDone, total: translatorTotal, pct: translatorPct },\\r\\n    gauntlet: { done: gauntletDone, total: gauntletTotal, pct: gauntletPct },\\r\\n    drills: { done: drillsDone, total: drillsTotal, pct: drillsPct },\\r\\n    flashcards: { done: flashcardsDone, total: flashcardsTotal, pct: flashcardsPct },\\r\\n    finalSet: { done: finalSetDone, total: finalSetTotal, pct: finalSetPct },\\r\\n    overallPct,\\r\\n  };\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\n// recommendNext: pick the surface with the lowest progress that still has\\r\\n// runway. Returns a tuple of (label, view-state, blurb) the dashboard can\\r\\n// render in the \\"Recommended next\\" tile.\\r\\nexport function recommendNext(p: ProgressBreakdown): {\\r\\n  label: string;\\r\\n  view: import(\\"../types\\").ViewState;\\r\\n  blurb: string;\\r\\n} {\\r\\n  const candidates: Array<{\\r\\n    key: keyof Omit<ProgressBreakdown, \\"overallPct\\">;\\r\\n    label: string;\\r\\n    view: import(\\"../types\\").ViewState;\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n      <p>\\r\\n        <strong>2. List what the stimulus must contain.</strong> One item per piece.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n      <p>\\r\\n        <strong>3. Point to each piece in the stimulus.</strong> Nouns are there, verbs happen, qualifiers have the\\r\\n        right strength.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n      <p>\\r\\n        <strong>4. Check the strong words.</strong> One word too strong sinks the choice.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n      <p>\\r\\n        The quiz gives you ten answer choices you haven\'t decoded yet. The drill gives you four full Flaw\\r\\n        questions. Run the routine on every choice, not just the first one that sounds right.\\r\\n      </p>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  );\\r\\n}\\r\\n\\r\\nexport const NOTE_COMPONENTS: Record<string, () => JSX.Element> = {\\r\\n  why_hard: WhyHardNote,\\r\\n  words_to_know: WordsNote,\\r\\n  deabstractifying_method: BreakItDownNote,\\r\\n  art_of_matching: RequirementMatchingLesson,\\r\\n  master_keys: ReasoningMoveLesson,\\r\\n  five_bucket_indicators: PreciseDescriptionLesson,\\r\\n  abstract_15: CompleteProcedureLesson,\\r\\n};\\r\\n"}'}]

[],\\r\\n            correctCount: r.correct_count ?? 0,\\r\\n            completedAt: r.completed_at,\\r\\n          })),\\r\\n          translatorAttempts: translators.map((r) => ({\\r\\n            stemNumber: r.stem_number,\\r\\n            keywordsPicked: r.keywords_picked ??
